PART I: FOUNDATIONAL CONCEPTS
================================================================================

1. WHAT IS PLAYWRIGHT?
======================

Definition:
-----------
Playwright is an open-source Node.js library developed by Microsoft that provides
a powerful, reliable API for automating Chromium, Firefox, and WebKit browsers
with a single unified interface.

Core Purpose:
-------------
Playwright solves critical challenges in modern web application testing:

1. Reliability Issues:
   - Eliminates flaky tests through built-in auto-waiting
   - Smart waiting for elements to be actionable
   - Automatic retries for assertions

2. Cross-Browser Compatibility:
   - Single API works across all major browser engines
   - Consistent behavior across Chromium, Firefox, and WebKit
   - Test once, run everywhere

3. Modern Web Support:
   - Handles SPAs (Single Page Applications)
   - Supports Shadow DOM and Web Components
   - Manages complex JavaScript-heavy applications

4. Speed and Performance:
   - Faster execution than traditional tools
   - Parallel test execution
   - Efficient browser automation protocols

5. Developer Experience:
   - Excellent debugging tools (Inspector, Trace Viewer)
   - TypeScript support out of the box
   - Comprehensive documentation and examples

Key Capabilities:
-----------------
✓ End-to-end testing across multiple browsers
✓ UI testing with visual regression
✓ API testing with built-in request context
✓ Mobile device emulation
✓ Network interception and mocking
✓ Screenshot and video recording
✓ Accessibility testing
✓ Performance monitoring

How Playwright Differs from Older Tools:
-----------------------------------------

vs. Selenium WebDriver:
- Faster execution (direct browser protocols vs HTTP)
- Built-in auto-waiting (no explicit waits needed)
- Better debugging tools
- Modern architecture designed for current web standards

vs. Cypress:
- True cross-browser support (Chromium, Firefox, WebKit)
- Better handling of iframes and multiple tabs
- No same-origin limitations
- Can test multiple domains in one test

Architecture Advantages:
------------------------
1. Direct Browser Control:
   - Uses DevTools Protocol (Chromium)
   - Custom protocols for Firefox and WebKit
   - No middleware or proxy servers

2. Out-of-Process Execution:
   - Node.js process controls browser
   - Doesn't run inside browser like Cypress
   - Can access both browser and Node.js APIs

3. Context Isolation:
   - Each test gets fresh browser context
   - Complete isolation between tests
   - No state pollution

Use Cases:
----------
• Automated regression testing
• Cross-browser compatibility testing
• End-to-end user journey validation
• Visual regression testing
• API testing alongside UI tests
• Performance monitoring
• Accessibility compliance testing
• Web scraping and automation

2. PLAYWRIGHT ARCHITECTURE
===========================

Official Architecture Reference:
---------------------------------
📚 **Official Playwright Architecture Diagram:**
https://playwright.dev/docs/library#browser-contexts

📚 **GitHub - Playwright Overview:**
https://github.com/microsoft/playwright#readme

📚 **Microsoft Playwright Blog - Architecture Deep Dive:**
https://devblogs.microsoft.com/playwright/

📚 **Playwright Documentation - Browser Contexts:**
https://playwright.dev/docs/browser-contexts

High-Level Architecture Overview:
----------------------------------

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                      YOUR TEST CODE                              │
│              (Node.js JavaScript/TypeScript)                     │
│                                                                   │
│  test('example', async ({ page }) => {                          │
│    await page.goto('https://example.com');                      │
│    await page.click('button');                                  │
│  });                                                             │
└────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────────────┘
                         │
                         │ Playwright API
                         ▼
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                   PLAYWRIGHT LIBRARY                             │
│                  (Node.js Library)                               │
│                                                                   │
│  ┌──────────────┐  ┌──────────────┐  ┌──────────────┐          │
│  │  Test Runner │  │   Fixtures   │  │  Assertions  │          │
│  └──────────────┘  └──────────────┘  └──────────────┘          │
│                                                                   │
│  ┌──────────────┐  ┌──────────────┐  ┌──────────────┐          │
│  │   Browser    │  │   Context    │  │     Page     │          │
│  │   Manager    │  │   Manager    │  │   Manager    │          │
│  └──────────────┘  └──────────────┘  └──────────────┘          │
└────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────────────┘
                         │
                         │ Browser Protocol
                         │
        ┌────────────────┼────────────────┐
        │                │                │
        ▼                ▼                ▼
┌──────────────┐ ┌──────────────┐ ┌──────────────┐
│   CHROMIUM   │ │   FIREFOX    │ │   WEBKIT     │
│   BROWSER    │ │   BROWSER    │ │   BROWSER    │
│              │ │              │ │              │
│   DevTools   │ │  Juggler     │ │  WebDriver   │
│   Protocol   │ │  Protocol    │ │  Protocol    │
└──────────────┘ └──────────────┘ └──────────────┘
```

Architecture Components Explained:
-----------------------------------

**Layer 1: Test Code (Your Code)**
```
┌─────────────────────────────────┐
│  Your Test Scripts              │
│  - test.spec.ts files           │
│  - Page Object Models           │
│  - Helper functions             │
│  - Configuration files          │
└─────────────────────────────────┘
```

What it does:
- Written in JavaScript/TypeScript
- Runs in Node.js environment
- Uses Playwright API to control browsers
- Defines test scenarios and assertions

Example:
```typescript
import { test, expect } from '@playwright/test';

test('user can login', async ({ page }) => {
  await page.goto('https://example.com/login');
  await page.fill('#username', 'user');
  await page.fill('#password', 'pass');
  await page.click('#submit');
  await expect(page).toHaveURL('/dashboard');
});
```

**Layer 2: Playwright Library (Core Engine)**
```
┌───────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Playwright Library (Node.js Package)        │
│                                               │
│  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
│  │  @playwright/test (Test Runner)         │ │
│  │  - Test discovery & execution           │ │
│  │  - Parallel execution                   │ │
│  │  - Fixtures & hooks                     │ │
│  │  - Reporters                            │ │
│  └─────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
│                                               │
│  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
│  │  playwright (Core Library)              │ │
│  │  - Browser launching                    │ │
│  │  - Context management                   │ │
│  │  - Page automation                      │ │
│  │  - Locators & selectors                 │ │
│  │  - Auto-waiting & retry logic           │ │
│  └─────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
└───────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

Components:

1. **Test Runner (@playwright/test)**
   - Discovers test files
   - Manages parallel execution with workers
   - Provides fixtures (page, context, browser)
   - Handles before/after hooks
   - Generates reports

2. **Browser Manager**
   - Downloads and maintains browser binaries
   - Launches browser processes
   - Manages browser lifecycle
   - Handles browser options and arguments

3. **Context Manager**
   - Creates isolated browser contexts
   - Manages cookies, storage, and cache
   - Handles authentication state
   - Provides context isolation per test

4. **Page Manager**
   - Controls individual pages/tabs
   - Manages navigation and lifecycle
   - Handles frames and iframes
   - Provides element interaction APIs

5. **Locator Engine**
   - Resolves element selectors
   - Implements auto-waiting
   - Handles auto-retry logic
   - Provides actionability checks

**Layer 3: Browser Communication (Protocols)**
```
┌────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Browser-Specific Protocols               │
│                                            │
│  Chromium:  Chrome DevTools Protocol      │
│  Firefox:   Juggler Protocol (Custom)     │
│  WebKit:    WebKit Protocol (Custom)      │
└────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

How it works:

1. **Chromium - DevTools Protocol (CDP)**
   - Industry-standard protocol
   - Same protocol Chrome DevTools uses
   - WebSocket-based communication
   - Rich API for debugging and automation

2. **Firefox - Juggler Protocol**
   - Custom protocol built by Playwright team
   - Integrated into Firefox
   - Similar capabilities to CDP
   - Ensures cross-browser consistency

3. **WebKit - WebKit Protocol**
   - Custom protocol for WebKit
   - Built into WebKit engine
   - Provides same API surface
   - Works on Safari technology

**Layer 4: Browser Processes**
```
┌──────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Browser Binary                          │
│                                          │
│  ┌────────────────────────────────────┐ │
│  │  Browser Process                   │ │
│  │  - Main browser process            │ │
│  │  - Window management               │ │
│  └────────────────────────────────────┘ │
│                                          │
│  ┌────────────────────────────────────┐ │
│  │  Renderer Processes                │ │
│  │  - One per page/context            │ │
│  │  - Executes web page code          │ │
│  │  - Renders DOM and CSS             │ │
│  └────────────────────────────────────┘ │
│                                          │
│  ┌────────────────────────────────────┐ │
│  │  GPU Process                       │ │
│  │  - Hardware acceleration           │ │
│  └────────────────────────────────────┘ │
└──────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

Browser → Context → Page Hierarchy:
------------------------------------

```
Browser Instance (Heavy)
├── Context 1 (Lightweight, Isolated)
│   ├── Page 1.1 (Tab/Window)
│   ├── Page 1.2 (Tab/Window)
│   └── Page 1.3 (Tab/Window)
│
├── Context 2 (Lightweight, Isolated)
│   ├── Page 2.1 (Tab/Window)
│   └── Page 2.2 (Tab/Window)
│
└── Context 3 (Lightweight, Isolated)
    └── Page 3.1 (Tab/Window)
```

**Browser:**
- One per worker process
- Heavy resource (spawns actual browser)
- Expensive to create (~seconds)
- Shared across multiple tests (reused)
- Contains browser settings and preferences

**Context:**
- Isolated browsing session (like incognito mode)
- Lightweight (milliseconds to create)
- Own cookies, storage, cache
- Own permissions and geolocation
- Playwright creates new context per test automatically
- Complete isolation between contexts

**Page:**
- Single tab/window within a context
- Represents one web page
- Where you interact with DOM
- Playwright provides one page per test via fixture
- Can create additional pages: context.newPage()

Data Flow Example:
------------------

```
Step 1: You write test code
┌───────────────────────────────────┐
│ await page.click('#submit')       │
└───────────┬───────────────────────┘
            │
Step 2: Playwright Library processes
            ▼
┌───────────────────────────────────┐
│ 1. Locate element with '#submit'  │
│ 2. Wait for element to be visible │
│ 3. Wait for element to be enabled │
│ 4. Scroll element into view       │
│ 5. Check element is actionable    │
└───────────┬───────────────────────┘
            │
Step 3: Send protocol command
            ▼
┌───────────────────────────────────┐
│ Protocol: DOM.getDocument         │
│ Protocol: DOM.querySelector       │
│ Protocol: Input.dispatchMouseEvent│
└───────────┬───────────────────────┘
            │
Step 4: Browser executes
            ▼
┌───────────────────────────────────┐
│ Browser finds element             │
│ Browser performs click            │
│ Browser triggers JavaScript events│
│ Browser updates DOM               │
└───────────┬───────────────────────┘
            │
Step 5: Result sent back
            ▼
┌───────────────────────────────────┐
│ Protocol: Success/Failure         │
│ Playwright: Resolves promise      │
│ Test: Continues to next step      │
└───────────────────────────────────┘
```

Test Execution Architecture:
-----------------------------

```
Main Process (Test Runner)
│
├── Worker 1 (Parallel)
│   ├── Browser Instance 1
│   │   └── Context 1 → Page 1 → Test A
│   │   └── Context 2 → Page 2 → Test B
│   │   └── Context 3 → Page 3 → Test C
│
├── Worker 2 (Parallel)
│   ├── Browser Instance 2
│   │   └── Context 1 → Page 1 → Test D
│   │   └── Context 2 → Page 2 → Test E
│
└── Worker 3 (Parallel)
    ├── Browser Instance 3
        └── Context 1 → Page 1 → Test F
        └── Context 2 → Page 2 → Test G
```

How it works:
1. **Main Process** discovers all tests
2. **Workers** are spawned (default: CPU cores / 2)
3. Each **Worker** launches one **Browser** instance
4. Each **Test** gets a fresh **Context** (isolated)
5. Each **Context** has one default **Page**
6. Tests run in **parallel** across workers
7. Contexts are **destroyed** after each test
8. Browser is **reused** for multiple tests within worker

Isolation Guarantees:
---------------------

```
Test 1                    Test 2
├── Fresh Context         ├── Fresh Context
│   ├── Clean cookies     │   ├── Clean cookies
│   ├── Empty storage     │   ├── Empty storage
│   ├── No cache          │   ├── No cache
│   └── Fresh page        │   └── Fresh page
│                         │
└── Cannot affect Test 2  └── Cannot affect Test 1
```

**Why Isolation Matters:**
- No test pollution
- Tests can run in any order
- Parallel execution is safe
- Deterministic test results

Network Interception Architecture:
-----------------------------------

```
Test Code
    │
    ├── page.route('**/api/*', route => ...)
    │
    ▼
Playwright Library
    │
    ├── Intercepts network at protocol level
    │
    ▼
Browser Protocol
    │
    ├── Network.requestWillBeSent
    ├── Network.responseReceived
    │
    ▼
Browser
    │
    ├── Request intercepted
    ├── Mock response returned
    └── Or request fulfilled/aborted
```

Key Architecture Benefits:
--------------------------

**1. Speed:**
```
Selenium:              Playwright:
Node.js                Node.js
  ↓ HTTP                ↓ WebSocket (faster)
WebDriver Server       Browser Protocol
  ↓ JSON Wire           ↓ Binary Protocol
Browser                Browser

Result: Playwright is 2-3x faster
```

**2. Reliability:**
- Auto-waiting built into protocol layer
- Element actionability checks at browser level
- Automatic retry logic for flaky selectors
- No race conditions

**3. Browser Coverage:**
- Single API works across all browsers
- Consistent behavior via protocol abstraction
- Same test code for Chromium, Firefox, WebKit

**4. Modern Features:**
- Network interception at protocol level
- Native browser contexts (fast isolation)
- Screenshot/video without external tools
- Trace recording at protocol level

File System Organization:
--------------------------

```
Your Project
├── node_modules/
│   ├── playwright/              ← Core library
│   │   ├── lib/                ← JavaScript code
│   │   ├── types/              ← TypeScript definitions
│   │   └── .local-browsers/    ← Browser binaries
│   │       ├── chromium-1234/
│   │       ├── firefox-5678/
│   │       └── webkit-9012/
│   │
│   └── @playwright/test/       ← Test runner
│       ├── lib/
│       └── reporter/
│
├── tests/                      ← Your tests
├── playwright.config.ts        ← Configuration
└── package.json               ← Dependencies
```

Architecture Summary - Key Takeaways:
--------------------------------------

✅ **Three-Layer Architecture:**
   1. Test Code (Your TypeScript/JavaScript)
   2. Playwright Library (Node.js)
   3. Browser (Chromium/Firefox/WebKit)

✅ **Communication:**
   - WebSocket-based protocols
   - Direct browser control
   - No middleware or proxy

✅ **Hierarchy:**
   - Browser (Heavy) → Context (Light) → Page (Single Tab)

✅ **Isolation:**
   - Each test gets fresh context
   - Complete isolation between tests
   - Safe parallel execution

✅ **Workers:**
   - Parallel test execution
   - One browser per worker
   - Multiple tests per browser (different contexts)

✅ **Protocols:**
   - Chromium: Chrome DevTools Protocol
   - Firefox: Juggler (Custom)
   - WebKit: WebKit Protocol (Custom)

✅ **Benefits:**
   - Fast execution (direct protocols)
   - Reliable (built-in waiting)
   - Cross-browser (unified API)
   - Modern (supports latest web features)

**Visual Architecture Reference URLs:**
```
📚 Official Docs - Architecture:
https://playwright.dev/docs/library

📚 GitHub - Technical Overview:
https://github.com/microsoft/playwright/blob/main/docs/src/library.md

📚 Browser Contexts Diagram:
https://playwright.dev/docs/browser-contexts

📚 Playwright vs Other Tools:
https://playwright.dev/docs/why-playwright
```

================================================================================

3. NODE.JS AND NPM FUNDAMENTALS
================================

Why Node.js is Required for Playwright:
----------------------------------------

Node.js Provides:

1. JavaScript Runtime Environment:
   - V8 JavaScript engine (same as Chrome)
   - Executes JavaScript outside the browser
   - Enables running test scripts as standalone applications

2. Module System:
   - CommonJS and ES Modules support
   - Organize code into reusable modules
   - Import/export functionality

3. Asynchronous Programming:
   - Event-driven, non-blocking I/O
   - Perfect for handling async browser operations
   - Native Promise and async/await support

4. Package Management:
   - npm (Node Package Manager) included
   - Install and manage dependencies
   - Version control for packages

5. Cross-Platform Compatibility:
   - Same code runs on Windows, macOS, Linux
   - Consistent behavior across operating systems

How Node.js Enables JavaScript Execution:
------------------------------------------

Traditional Web (Browser):
HTML → JavaScript Engine (V8) → Web APIs → DOM Manipulation

Node.js (Server/CLI):
JavaScript Code → V8 Engine → Node.js APIs → File System/Network/OS

For Playwright:
Test Script → Node.js (V8) → Playwright Library → Browser Protocols → Browser

Process Flow:
1. You write: test('example', async ({ page }) => { ... })
2. Node.js executes the test function
3. Playwright APIs communicate with browser
4. Browser performs actions
5. Results return to Node.js
6. Assertions verify outcomes

NPM (Node Package Manager):
----------------------------

What npm Does:

1. Package Installation:
   npm install playwright
   npm install @playwright/test
   npm install --save-dev typescript

2. Dependency Management:
   - Reads package.json
   - Installs all listed dependencies
   - Creates/updates package-lock.json
   - Ensures version consistency

3. Script Execution:
   - Defines scripts in package.json
   - npm test, npm run build
   - Simplifies command execution

4. Version Control:
   - Semantic versioning (^1.40.0)
   - Locks exact versions in package-lock.json
   - Prevents "works on my machine" issues

5. Browser Binary Management:
   - Playwright uses npm to download browsers
   - Stores in specific cache location
   - Manages multiple versions

NPX (Node Package Executor):
-----------------------------

What is npx:
- Comes bundled with npm (since 5.2.0)
- Executes packages without global installation
- Runs local or remote packages

How npx Works:
1. Checks node_modules/.bin/ for package
2. If found, runs local version
3. If not found, downloads temporarily
4. Executes and optionally discards

Benefits:
✓ No global installation pollution
✓ Always uses project-specific version
✓ Try tools without permanent install
✓ Essential for CI/CD consistency

Common npx Commands with Playwright:
npx playwright test              # Run tests
npx playwright test --ui         # UI mode
npx playwright codegen           # Generate tests
npx playwright install           # Install browsers
npx npm init playwright@latest   # Initialize project

Why This Matters:
- Team members use same Playwright version
- CI/CD runs exact version specified
- No conflicts between project versions
- Clean development environment

package.json vs package-lock.json:
----------------------------------

package.json:
- Defines project dependencies
- Specifies version ranges (^, ~)
- Contains project metadata
- Defines npm scripts
- Manually editable

package-lock.json:
- Locks exact versions of all dependencies
- Auto-generated and maintained
- Ensures reproducible installs
- Should be committed to git
- Don't edit manually

Example package.json:
{
  "name": "my-playwright-tests",
  "version": "1.0.0",
  "scripts": {
    "test": "playwright test",
    "test:headed": "playwright test --headed",
    "test:debug": "playwright test --debug"
  },
  "devDependencies": {
    "@playwright/test": "^1.40.0",
    "@types/node": "^20.10.0"
  }
}

================================================================================

3. INSTALLATION AND SETUP
==========================

Prerequisites:
--------------
1. Node.js installed (version 16 or higher)
   - Download from nodejs.org
   - Verify: node --version
   - Verify: npm --version

Initial Setup - Step by Step:
------------------------------

Step 1: Create Project Directory
mkdir my-playwright-project
cd my-playwright-project

Step 2: Initialize Playwright Project
npm init playwright@latest

Interactive Prompts:
? Do you want to use TypeScript or JavaScript? › TypeScript (recommended)
? Where to put your end-to-end tests? › tests
? Add a GitHub Actions workflow? › false (initially)
? Install Playwright browsers? › true

Step 3: Understanding What Gets Installed

Files Created:
my-playwright-project/
├── tests/
│   └── example.spec.ts
├── tests-examples/
│   └── demo-todo-app.spec.ts
├── playwright.config.ts
├── package.json
├── package-lock.json
├── .gitignore
└── node_modules/

Browser Binaries Downloaded:
~/.cache/ms-playwright/           (Linux/Mac)
%USERPROFILE%\AppData\Local\ms-playwright\  (Windows)

Contains:
- chromium-<version>/
- firefox-<version>/
- webkit-<version>/

Step 4: Verify Installation

Run example tests:
npx playwright test

View HTML report:
npx playwright show-report

Run in headed mode:
npx playwright test --headed

Run in debug mode:
npx playwright test --debug

Browser Management:
-------------------

Why Playwright Downloads Its Own Browsers:

1. Version Consistency:
   - Same browser version on all machines
   - Dev, CI, and production use identical browsers
   - No "works on my machine" issues

2. Optimized Builds:
   - Special builds patched for Playwright
   - Enhanced automation capabilities
   - Faster and more reliable

3. Isolation:
   - Doesn't interfere with system browsers
   - System browser updates don't break tests
   - Can test specific browser versions

4. Complete Control:
   - Test against older browser versions
   - Pin specific versions for stability
   - Manage updates explicitly

Browser Installation Commands:

Install all browsers:
npx playwright install

Install specific browser:
npx playwright install chromium
npx playwright install firefox
npx playwright install webkit

Install with system dependencies (Linux):
npx playwright install --with-deps

Install for specific Playwright version:
npx playwright install --force

Check installed browsers:
npx playwright install --help

Troubleshooting Installation:
------------------------------

Common Issues:

1. Network/Firewall Problems:
   Error: Failed to download browser
   
   Solutions:
   - Check internet connection
   - Configure proxy settings:
     HTTP_PROXY=http://proxy:8080 npx playwright install
   - Download manually from CDN
   - Check corporate firewall settings

2. Permissions Issues:
   Error: EACCES permission denied
   
   Solutions:
   - Don't use sudo with npm
   - Fix npm permissions: npm config set prefix ~/.npm-global
   - On Windows: Run as administrator (if necessary)

3. Disk Space:
   Error: No space left on device
   
   Solutions:
   - Free up disk space (browsers ~1GB total)
   - Clean npm cache: npm cache clean --force
   - Remove old Playwright versions

4. Version Conflicts:
   Error: Incompatible browser version
   
   Solutions:
   - Update Playwright: npm install @playwright/test@latest
   - Reinstall browsers: npx playwright install --force
   - Check package.json versions

Debugging Installation:
DEBUG=pw:install npx playwright install

Alternative Installation Methods:
----------------------------------

1. Add to Existing Project:
   npm install --save-dev @playwright/test
   npx playwright install

2. Install Specific Version:
   npm install --save-dev @playwright/test@1.40.0
   npx playwright install

3. Use Yarn:
   yarn add --dev @playwright/test
   yarn playwright install

4. Use pnpm:
   pnpm add -D @playwright/test
   pnpm exec playwright install

================================================================================

4. PROJECT STRUCTURE AND CONFIGURATION
=======================================

Default Folder Structure Explained:
------------------------------------

Complete Structure:
my-playwright-project/
├── .github/
│   └── workflows/
│       └── playwright.yml          # CI/CD workflow (optional)
├── node_modules/                   # npm packages (auto-generated)
│   └── @playwright/
│       └── test/
├── test-results/                   # Test output (auto-generated)
│   ├── example-chromium/
│   │   ├── video.webm
│   │   └── trace.zip
│   └── test-failed/
│       └── screenshot.png
├── playwright-report/              # HTML reports (auto-generated)
│   └── index.html
├── tests/                          # YOUR TEST FILES
│   ├── example.spec.ts
│   ├── login/
│   │   ├── login.spec.ts
│   │   └── logout.spec.ts
│   ├── e2e/
│   │   └── checkout.spec.ts
│   └── api/
│       └── users.api.spec.ts
├── tests-examples/                 # Playwright examples (optional)
│   └── demo-todo-app.spec.ts
├── pages/                          # Page Objects (you create)
│   ├── LoginPage.ts
│   └── DashboardPage.ts
├── fixtures/                       # Custom fixtures (you create)
│   └── customFixtures.ts
├── utils/                          # Helper functions (you create)
│   └── testHelpers.ts
├── playwright.config.ts            # Main configuration
├── .env                            # Environment variables (create if needed)
├── .env.example                    # Example env file
├── .gitignore                      # Git ignore rules
├── package.json                    # Project manifest
├── package-lock.json               # Locked dependencies
├── tsconfig.json                   # TypeScript config (if using TS)
└── README.md                       # Project documentation

Directory Purposes:
-------------------

1. tests/
   Purpose: Your actual test files
   Naming: *.spec.ts or *.test.ts
   Organization:
   - Group by feature: tests/login/, tests/checkout/
   - Group by type: tests/e2e/, tests/api/, tests/visual/
   - Both: tests/features/login/login.e2e.spec.ts

2. tests-examples/
   Purpose: Learning examples from Playwright
   Management:
   - Delete once familiar
   - Or ignore in config: testIgnore: '**/tests-examples/**'
   - Don't commit if not needed

3. test-results/
   Purpose: Test execution artifacts
   Contains:
   - Screenshots (on failure)
   - Videos (if enabled)
   - Traces (for debugging)
   Git: Add to .gitignore
   CI: Upload as artifacts

4. playwright-report/
   Purpose: HTML test reports
   View: npx playwright show-report
   Git: Add to .gitignore
   CI: Publish as GitHub Pages or artifacts

5. pages/ (Page Object Model)
   Purpose: Page object classes
   Pattern:
   export class LoginPage {
     constructor(private page: Page) {}
     
     async login(username, password) {
       await this.page.fill('#username', username);
       await this.page.fill('#password', password);
       await this.page.click('#login');
     }
   }

6. fixtures/
   Purpose: Custom test fixtures
   Example:
   export const test = base.extend<{
     authenticatedPage: Page;
   }>({
     authenticatedPage: async ({ page }, use) => {
       // Login logic
       await use(page);
     }
   });

7. utils/
   Purpose: Shared helper functions
   Examples:
   - Data generators
   - Common assertions
   - API helpers
   - Database utilities

playwright.config.ts - Complete Breakdown:
-------------------------------------------

Essential Configuration File:

import { defineConfig, devices } from '@playwright/test';

export default defineConfig({
  // Test directory
  testDir: './tests',
  
  // Test file patterns
  testMatch: '**/*.spec.ts',
  
  // Ignore patterns
  testIgnore: '**/tests-examples/**',
  
  // Global timeout per test
  timeout: 30000,  // 30 seconds
  
  // Maximum time for all tests
  globalTimeout: 60 * 60 * 1000,  // 1 hour
  
  // Expect timeout (for assertions)
  expect: {
    timeout: 5000  // 5 seconds
  },
  
  // Run tests in parallel
  fullyParallel: true,
  
  // Fail fast on CI
  forbidOnly: !!process.env.CI,
  
  // Retry failed tests
  retries: process.env.CI ? 2 : 0,
  
  // Number of parallel workers
  workers: process.env.CI ? 2 : undefined,
  
  // Reporter configuration
  reporter: [
    ['list'],                                    // Console output
    ['html', { outputFolder: 'playwright-report' }],  // HTML report
    ['json', { outputFile: 'test-results.json' }],    // JSON report
    ['junit', { outputFile: 'junit.xml' }]            // JUnit XML
  ],
  
  // Global setup/teardown
  globalSetup: require.resolve('./global-setup'),
  globalTeardown: require.resolve('./global-teardown'),
  
  // Shared settings for all tests
  use: {
    // Base URL for navigation
    baseURL: process.env.BASE_URL || 'http://localhost:3000',
    
    // Browser options
    headless: true,
    
    // Viewport size
    viewport: { width: 1280, height: 720 },
    
    // User agent
    // userAgent: 'custom-user-agent',
    
    // Ignore HTTPS errors
    ignoreHTTPSErrors: true,
    
    // Accept downloads
    acceptDownloads: true,
    
    // Screenshot on failure
    screenshot: 'only-on-failure',
    
    // Video recording
    video: 'retain-on-failure',
    
    // Trace on retry
    trace: 'on-first-retry',
    
    // Action timeout
    actionTimeout: 10000,
    
    // Navigation timeout
    navigationTimeout: 30000,
    
    // Slow down actions (for debugging)
    // launchOptions: { slowMo: 1000 }
  },
  
  // Browser projects
  projects: [
    {
      name: 'chromium',
      use: { ...devices['Desktop Chrome'] },
    },
    {
      name: 'firefox',
      use: { ...devices['Desktop Firefox'] },
    },
    {
      name: 'webkit',
      use: { ...devices['Desktop Safari'] },
    },
    
    // Mobile browsers
    {
      name: 'Mobile Chrome',
      use: { ...devices['Pixel 5'] },
    },
    {
      name: 'Mobile Safari',
      use: { ...devices['iPhone 12'] },
    },
    
    // Branded browsers
    {
      name: 'Microsoft Edge',
      use: { 
        ...devices['Desktop Edge'], 
        channel: 'msedge' 
      },
    },
    {
      name: 'Google Chrome',
      use: { 
        ...devices['Desktop Chrome'], 
        channel: 'chrome' 
      },
    },
    
    // Setup project (for authentication)
    {
      name: 'setup',
      testMatch: '**/*.setup.ts',
    },
    
    // Authenticated tests
    {
      name: 'authenticated',
      use: { 
        storageState: 'auth.json' 
      },
      dependencies: ['setup'],
    }
  ],
  
  // Web server (for local development)
  webServer: {
    command: 'npm run start',
    url: 'http://localhost:3000',
    reuseExistingServer: !process.env.CI,
    timeout: 120000,
  },
});

Configuration Patterns:
-----------------------

1. Environment-Based Configuration:

const config = {
  dev: {
    baseURL: 'http://localhost:3000',
    workers: undefined,  // Use all cores
  },
  staging: {
    baseURL: 'https://staging.example.com',
    workers: 4,
  },
  production: {
    baseURL: 'https://example.com',
    workers: 2,
  }
};

const env = process.env.ENV || 'dev';

export default defineConfig({
  use: {
    baseURL: config[env].baseURL,
  },
  workers: config[env].workers,
});

2. Multiple Configuration Files:

playwright.config.ts         # Default
playwright.config.dev.ts     # Development
playwright.config.ci.ts      # CI/CD

Run with specific config:
npx playwright test --config=playwright.config.ci.ts

Test Discovery:
---------------

How Playwright Finds Tests:

1. Starts from testDir: './tests'
2. Recursively searches subdirectories
3. Matches files against testMatch pattern
4. Excludes files matching testIgnore
5. Loads and executes matched files

Customizing Discovery:

export default defineConfig({
  testDir: './e2e-tests',
  testMatch: '**/*-test.ts',
  testIgnore: ['**/helpers/**', '**/fixtures/**'],
});

Best Practices for Organization:
---------------------------------

1. Feature-Based Structure:
   tests/
   ├── auth/
   │   ├── login.spec.ts
   │   ├── signup.spec.ts
   │   └── password-reset.spec.ts
   ├── products/
   │   ├── product-list.spec.ts
   │   └── product-details.spec.ts
   └── checkout/
       └── checkout-flow.spec.ts

2. Type-Based Structure:
   tests/
   ├── e2e/
   ├── integration/
   ├── api/
   └── visual/

3. Hybrid Approach:
   tests/
   ├── features/
   │   └── shopping-cart/
   │       ├── add-to-cart.e2e.spec.ts
   │       ├── remove-from-cart.e2e.spec.ts
   │       └── cart.api.spec.ts
   └── smoke/
       └── critical-paths.spec.ts

================================================================================

5. IMPORT STATEMENT DEEP DIVE
==============================

The Essential Import:
---------------------
import { test, expect } from '@playwright/test';

Complete Breakdown:
-------------------

1. 'import' Keyword (ES6 Module Syntax):
   - Introduced in ECMAScript 2015 (ES6)
   - Loads modules at compile time (static imports)
   - Creates immutable bindings (can't reassign imported items)
   - Tree-shaking friendly (unused imports removed in build)

Alternative: Dynamic Imports (for conditional loading)
const { test } = await import('@playwright/test');

2. Destructuring Syntax { test, expect }:
   - Extracts specific named exports
   - More efficient than importing everything
   - Makes dependencies explicit
   - Enables better IDE autocomplete

What You're Actually Importing:

a) test Function:
   - Type: Function
   - Purpose: Define test cases
   - Returns: void
   - Signature: test(title: string, testFunction: Function)
   
   Variants:
   - test.describe(): Group tests
   - test.beforeEach(): Setup before each test
   - test.afterEach(): Cleanup after each test
   - test.only(): Run only this test
   - test.skip(): Skip this test
   - test.fixme(): Mark as broken
   - test.slow(): Mark as slow test
   - test.fail(): Expect test to fail
   - test.step(): Add test steps

b) expect Function:
   - Type: Assertion Function
   - Purpose: Make assertions
   - Returns: Assertion object with matchers
   - Auto-waiting: Yes (retries until timeout)
   
   Common Matchers:
   - toHaveText()
   - toBeVisible()
   - toHaveURL()
   - toHaveTitle()
   - toContainText()
   - toHaveCount()
   - toBeEnabled()
   - toBeChecked()
   - toHaveAttribute()
   - toHaveValue()

3. 'from @playwright/test':
   - Module specifier
   - @ prefix indicates scoped package
   - @playwright = organization scope
   - /test = specific package within scope
   
   Resolution:
   Node.js looks in:
   1. node_modules/@playwright/test/
   2. Reads package.json → main or exports field
   3. Loads the specified entry point

Additional Imports:
-------------------

Full Import Options:

// Basic imports
import { test, expect } from '@playwright/test';

// Import types (TypeScript)
import { test, expect, Page, Browser, BrowserContext } from '@playwright/test';

// Import devices
import { test, expect, devices } from '@playwright/test';

// Import configuration helpers
import { defineConfig, PlaywrightTestConfig } from '@playwright/test';

// Import test result types
import { TestInfo, TestResult } from '@playwright/test';

Example with Types:

import { test, expect, Page, Locator } from '@playwright/test';

test('typed example', async ({ page }: { page: Page }) => {
  const button: Locator = page.locator('button');
  await button.click();
  await expect(button).toBeDisabled();
});

Extending Imports:
------------------

Custom Test with Fixtures:

import { test as base, expect } from '@playwright/test';

type MyFixtures = {
  adminPage: Page;
};

export const test = base.extend<MyFixtures>({
  adminPage: async ({ page }, use) => {
    await page.goto('/admin');
    await use(page);
  }
});

export { expect };

// Use in tests:
import { test, expect } from './fixtures/myTest';

test('admin test', async ({ adminPage }) => {
  await expect(adminPage).toHaveTitle('Admin');
});

Import Best Practices:
----------------------

1. Only Import What You Need:
   ✓ import { test, expect } from '@playwright/test';
   ✗ import * as playwright from '@playwright/test';

2. Group Related Imports:
   // Playwright imports
   import { test, expect, Page } from '@playwright/test';
   
   // Node.js imports
   import fs from 'fs';
   import path from 'path';
   
   // Custom imports
   import { LoginPage } from './pages/LoginPage';
   import { testData } from './fixtures/testData';

3. Use Type Imports (TypeScript):
   import type { Page, Locator } from '@playwright/test';
   // Only imports types, removed at runtime

4. Consistent Import Order:
   1. External packages
   2. Playwright imports
   3. Internal modules
   4. Page objects
   5. Fixtures
   6. Utilities

Behind the Scenes:
------------------

When you write:
import { test, expect } from '@playwright/test';

Node.js does:
1. Resolves module path
2. Checks module cache
3. Loads module (if not cached)
4. Executes module code
5. Returns exported values
6. Caches for future imports

The @playwright/test package exports:
{
  test: [Function: test] { extend: [Function], ... },
  expect: [Function: expect] { soft: [Function], ... },
  defineConfig: [Function: defineConfig],
  devices: { ... },
  // ... many more exports
}

================================================================================

6. TEST FUNCTION ANATOMY
=========================

Complete Test Function Structure:
----------------------------------

test('descriptive test name', async ({ page }) => {
  // Test implementation
});

Breaking Down Each Part:
------------------------

1. test( ... )
   - Function call to Playwright's test runner
   - Registers test with the framework
   - Isolated execution context
   - Auto cleanup after completion

2. 'descriptive test name'
   - First parameter: string
   - Test identifier in reports
   - Should describe what is being tested
   
   Good Names:
   ✓ 'should login with valid credentials'
   ✓ 'displays error for empty email field'
   ✓ 'adds product to cart and updates badge count'
   
   Bad Names:
   ✗ 'test1'
   ✗ 'login'
   ✗ 'works'

3. async
   - Marks function as asynchronous
   - Enables use of await keyword
   - Returns Promise automatically
   - Essential for Playwright's async operations

4. ({ page })
   - Parameter with object destructuring
   - Receives fixtures from Playwright
   - page is most common fixture
   - Can request multiple fixtures
   
   Examples:
   async ({ page })                              // Just page
   async ({ page, context })                     // Page and context
   async ({ page, browser, context })            // Multiple fixtures
   async ({ page, request })                     // Page and API client

5. => { }
   - Arrow function syntax
   - Function body contains test logic
   - Alternative: async function({ page }) { }
   - Arrow functions more concise

Available Fixtures:
-------------------

Built-in Fixtures:

1. page: Page
   - Fresh browser page/tab
   - Most commonly used
   - Auto-created and auto-cleaned
   
2. context: BrowserContext
   - Browser context (isolated session)
   - Create multiple pages
   - Share cookies/storage across pages
   
3. browser: Browser
   - Browser instance
   - Create custom contexts
   - Low-level browser control
   
4. request: APIRequestContext
   - API testing
   - HTTP requests
   - Independent of browser
   
5. browserName: string
   - 'chromium', 'firefox', or 'webkit'
   - Current browser name
   - Conditional test logic

Complete Test Example:
----------------------

import { test, expect } from '@playwright/test';

test.describe('Login Feature', () => {
  
  test.beforeEach(async ({ page }) => {
    // Runs before each test
    await page.goto('/login');
  });
  
  test('should login successfully with valid credentials', async ({ page }) => {
    // Arrange
    const username = 'testuser';
    const password = 'SecurePass123!';
    
    // Act
    await page.fill('#username', username);
    await page.fill('#password', password);
    await page.click('#login-button');
    
    // Assert
    await expect(page).toHaveURL('/dashboard');
    await expect(page.locator('.welcome-message')).toHaveText(`Welcome, ${username}!`);
  });
  
  test('should show error with invalid credentials', async ({ page }) => {
    // Act
    await page.fill('#username', 'wronguser');
    await page.fill('#password', 'wrongpass');
    await page.click('#login-button');
    
    // Assert
    await expect(page.locator('.error-message')).toBeVisible();
    await expect(page.locator('.error-message')).toHaveText('Invalid credentials');
    await expect(page).toHaveURL('/login');  // Still on login page
  });
  
  test.afterEach(async ({ page }) => {
    // Runs after each test
    await page.context().clearCookies();
  });
});

Test Modifiers:
---------------

test.only():
- Runs only this test (all others skipped)
- Useful for debugging specific test
- ⚠️ Remove before committing!

test.only('debug this test', async ({ page }) => {
  // Only this runs
});

test.skip():
- Skips this test
- Shows as skipped in report
- Add reason in comment

test.skip('not ready yet', async ({ page }) => {
  // Skipped
});

Conditional skip:
test.skip(browserName === 'webkit', 'Safari not supported');

test.fixme():
- Marks test as broken/failing
- Acknowledges known issue
- Different from skip (implies will be fixed)

test.fixme('has a bug (#123)', async ({ page }) => {
  // Known to fail
});

test.slow():
- Triples the timeout for this test
- Use for genuinely slow operations
- Don't overuse!

test.slow('generates large report', async ({ page }) => {
  // Gets 3x normal timeout
});

test.fail():
- Expects test to fail
- Inverts result (fail = pass)
- Temporary workaround

test.fail('known browser bug', async ({ page }) => {
  // Expected to fail
});

Test Annotations:
-----------------

test('test with metadata', {
  annotation: { type: 'issue', description: 'https://github.com/microsoft/playwright/issues/123' },
}, async ({ page }) => {
  // Test code
});

Multiple annotations:
test('test', {
  annotation: [
    { type: 'issue', description: 'Bug #123' },
    { type: 'performance', description: 'Slow query' },
  ],
}, async ({ page }) => {
  // Test code
});

Test Tags (Playwright 1.42+):
------------------------------

test('login @smoke @critical', async ({ page }) => {
  // Test code
});

Run tagged tests:
npx playwright test --grep @smoke
npx playwright test --grep "@smoke|@critical"
npx playwright test --grep-invert @slow

================================================================================

7. ASYNC/AWAIT MASTERY
=======================

Why Asynchronous Programming:
------------------------------

Web Automation is Inherently Async:
1. Network requests take time
2. Pages load progressively
3. Elements render dynamically
4. User interactions have delays
5. API calls are non-instant

The Problem Without Async:
---------------------------

Synchronous (Imaginary):
```
page.goto('https://example.com');  // Starts navigation
page.click('#button');              // Tries immediately - FAILS!
// Button doesn't exist yet because page not loaded
```

This creates:
- Race conditions
- Flaky tests
- Unreliable results
- "Element not found" errors

The Solution: async/await
--------------------------

async Keyword:
--------------

What it does:
1. Marks function as asynchronous
2. Enables await inside function
3. Always returns a Promise
4. Wraps return value in Promise.resolve()

Example:
async function example() {
  return "Hello";  // Returns Promise<string>
}

// Equivalent to:
function example() {
  return Promise.resolve("Hello");
}

await Keyword:
--------------

What it does:
1. Pauses execution until Promise resolves
2. Unwraps Promise value
3. Only works inside async functions
4. Makes async code look synchronous

Example:
const result = await someAsyncFunction();
console.log(result);  // result is unwrapped value, not Promise

How It Works - Step by Step:
-----------------------------

Example Test:
```
test('login flow', async ({ page }) => {
  await page.goto('https://example.com/login');     // Line 1
  await page.fill('#username', 'user');              // Line 2
  await page.fill('#password', 'pass');              // Line 3
  await page.click('#login');                        // Line 4
  await expect(page).toHaveURL('/dashboard');        // Line 5
});
```

Execution Flow:

Line 1: await page.goto()
- Sends navigation command to browser
- Execution pauses
- Browser loads page
- Promise resolves when page loaded
- Execution continues to Line 2

Line 2: await page.fill('#username', 'user')
- Waits for input to be available
- Waits for input to be visible
- Waits for input to be editable
- Types 'user'
- Execution continues to Line 3

Line 3: await page.fill('#password', 'pass')
- Same waiting process
- Types 'pass'
- Continues to Line 4

Line 4: await page.click('#login')
- Waits for button to be clickable
- Clicks button
- Waits for click to complete
- Continues to Line 5

Line 5: await expect(page).toHaveURL('/dashboard')
- Repeatedly checks URL (auto-retry)
- Waits up to timeout (default 5 seconds)
- Either passes or fails
- Test ends

Node.js Event Loop:
-------------------

How Node.js Handles Async:

1. Call Stack:
   - Executes synchronous code
   - Delegates async operations

2. Web APIs / C++ APIs:
   - Handle async operations
   - (Network, timers, file I/O)

3. Callback Queue:
   - Stores completed async results

4. Event Loop:
   - Moves callbacks to call stack
   - When call stack is empty

Example Flow:
```
console.log('1');                              // Stack
await page.goto('url');                        // Delegated → Web API
console.log('2');                              // Won't run until goto completes
```

Output:
1
[browser loads]
2

Benefits of async/await:
------------------------

1. Readability:
   Without async/await (Promise chains):
   ```
   page.goto('url')
     .then(() => page.fill('#username', 'user'))
     .then(() => page.fill('#password', 'pass'))
     .then(() => page.click('#login'))
     .then(() => expect(page).toHaveURL('/dashboard'));
   ```
   
   With async/await:
   ```
   await page.goto('url');
   await page.fill('#username', 'user');
   await page.fill('#password', 'pass');
   await page.click('#login');
   await expect(page).toHaveURL('/dashboard');
   ```
   Much more readable!

2. Error Handling:
   Without async/await:
   ```
   page.goto('url')
     .then(() => page.click('#button'))
     .catch(error => console.error(error));
   ```
   
   With async/await:
   ```
   try {
     await page.goto('url');
     await page.click('#button');
   } catch (error) {
     console.error(error);
   }
   ```

3. Debugging:
   - Can set breakpoints normally
   - Step through code line by line
   - Call stack makes sense

Common Patterns:
----------------

1. Sequential Operations:
```
await page.goto('/login');
await page.fill('#username', 'user');
await page.fill('#password', 'pass');
await page.click('#login');
```

2. Parallel Operations (when independent):
```
const [response1, response2] = await Promise.all([
  page.waitForResponse('**/api/user'),
  page.waitForResponse('**/api/settings')
]);
```

3. Conditional Async:
```
const isVisible = await page.locator('#modal').isVisible();
if (isVisible) {
  await page.click('#close-modal');
}
```

4. Loops with Async:
```
const items = await page.locator('.item').all();
for (const item of items) {
  await item.click();
  await expect(item).toHaveClass(/selected/);
}
```

5. Error Handling:
```
try {
  await page.goto('https://example.com');
  await page.click('#button');
} catch (error) {
  console.error('Navigation or click failed:', error);
  throw error;  // Re-throw if test should fail
}
```

What Needs await:
-----------------

Requires await:
✓ page.goto()
✓ page.click()
✓ page.fill()
✓ page.waitForSelector()
✓ expect(locator).toBeVisible()
✓ locator.textContent()
✓ locator.count()

Doesn't require await (synchronous):
✗ page.locator()  // Just creates locator, doesn't search
✗ page.url()      // Synchronous getter
✗ test.describe()
✗ test.beforeEach() // The hook itself, but content needs await

Common Mistakes:
----------------

1. Forgetting await:
```
// ❌ Wrong - returns Promise, doesn't wait
page.goto('url');
page.click('#button');  // Runs immediately, page not loaded!

// ✓ Correct
await page.goto('url');
await page.click('#button');
```

2. Awaiting non-Promise:
```
// ⚠️ Unnecessary but harmless
await page.locator('#button');  // locator() is synchronous

// ✓ Better
const button = page.locator('#button');
await button.click();  // This needs await
```

3. Not handling errors:
```
// ❌ Silent failure possible
await page.click('#button').catch(() => {});

// ✓ Let it fail or handle explicitly
try {
  await page.click('#button');
} catch (error) {
  // Explicit handling
  console.log('Button not found, trying alternative');
  await page.click('#alt-button');
}
```

Advanced: Promise.all() for Parallel:
--------------------------------------

When operations don't depend on each other:

Sequential (slow):
```
await page.fill('#field1', 'value1');  // 100ms
await page.fill('#field2', 'value2');  // 100ms
await page.fill('#field3', 'value3');  // 100ms
// Total: 300ms
```

Parallel (fast):
```
await Promise.all([
  page.fill('#field1', 'value1'),
  page.fill('#field2', 'value2'),
  page.fill('#field3', 'value3')
]);
// Total: ~100ms (all at once)
```

⚠️ Use carefully - operations must be truly independent!

Performance Example:
```
// Slow: 3 seconds (sequential)
await page.waitForResponse('**/api/user');
await page.waitForResponse('**/api/posts');
await page.waitForResponse('**/api/comments');

// Fast: ~1 second (parallel)
await Promise.all([
  page.waitForResponse('**/api/user'),
  page.waitForResponse('**/api/posts'),
  page.waitForResponse('**/api/comments')
]);
```

================================================================================

8. BROWSER CONTEXT AND ISOLATION
=================================

The Isolation Challenge:
------------------------

Scenario: Banking Application Tests

Test 1: Login as User A
Test 2: Transfer funds
Test 3: Logout and login as User B

Problems without isolation:
- Test 2 depends on Test 1's login
- Test 3 sees User A's session
- Tests can't run in parallel
- Random execution order breaks tests
- Flaky, unreliable results

What is a Browser Context?
---------------------------

Definition:
A Browser Context is like an incognito/private browsing session - a completely
isolated browsing environment within a browser instance.

Each Context Has Its Own:
1. Cookies
2. Local Storage
3. Session Storage
4. Cache
5. Permissions (geolocation, notifications, etc.)
6. Authentication state
7. Service Workers

Hierarchy:
Browser
└── Context 1 (isolated)
    ├── Page 1
    ├── Page 2
    └── Page 3
└── Context 2 (isolated)
    ├── Page 1
    └── Page 2

Key Point:
Actions in Context 1 don't affect Context 2!

Playwright's Automatic Isolation:
----------------------------------

What Playwright Does Per Test:

Before Test Starts:
1. Uses existing browser OR launches new one
2. Creates BRAND NEW browser context
3. Creates fresh page within that context
4. Injects page into test via fixture
5. Test runs with pristine state

After Test Ends:
1. Closes the page
2. Closes and destroys the context
3. All state (cookies, storage) wiped clean
4. Browser may stay open for next test
5. But new context = complete isolation

Visual Example:
```
Browser Instance (stays open)
│
├── Test 1 Execution
│   └── Context A (created)
│       └── Page (login, do stuff)
│   └── Context A (destroyed) ← All state gone!
│
├── Test 2 Execution
│   └── Context B (brand new)
│       └── Page (fresh start)
│   └── Context B (destroyed)
│
└── Test 3 Execution
    └── Context C (brand new)
        └── Page (no previous state)
    └── Context C (destroyed)
```

Real-World Example:
-------------------

Banking Application Tests:

```
import { test, expect } from '@playwright/test';

test('User A: Successful login', async ({ page }) => {
  // Playwright creates Context 1
  await page.goto('https://bank.example.com/login');
  await page.fill('#username', 'userA');
  await page.fill('#password', 'passA');
  await page.click('#login');
  
  await expect(page).toHaveURL('/dashboard');
  await expect(page.locator('.welcome')).toHaveText('Welcome, User A!');
  
  // User A is logged in within THIS context
});
// Playwright destroys Context 1 - User A session GONE!

test('User A: Transfer funds', async ({ page }) => {
  // Playwright creates Context 2 (completely fresh!)
  // NO USER A SESSION!
  // Must login again:
  
  await page.goto('https://bank.example.com/login');
  await page.fill('#username', 'userA');
  await page.fill('#password', 'passA');
  await page.click('#login');
  
  // Now do transfer
  await page.goto('/transfer');
  await page.fill('#amount', '100');
  await page.click('#transfer-button');
  
  await expect(page.locator('.success')).toBeVisible();
});
// Context 2 destroyed

test('Invalid login attempt', async ({ page }) => {
  // Context 3 (brand new)
  // No cookies from User A
  // No login state
  
  await page.goto('https://bank.example.com/login');
  await page.fill('#username', 'wronguser');
  await page.fill('#password', 'wrongpass');
  await page.click('#login');
  
  await expect(page.locator('.error')).toHaveText('Invalid credentials');
  await expect(page).toHaveURL('/login');
});
// Context 3 destroyed
```

Benefits of Automatic Isolation:
---------------------------------

1. Test Independence:
   - Each test starts from known state
   - No dependencies on other tests
   - Tests can run in any order

2. Parallel Execution:
   - Tests don't interfere with each other
   - Can run simultaneously
   - Faster test completion

3. Reliability:
   - No flaky tests from state pollution
   - Predictable, reproducible results
   - If test fails, it's the test or app, not environment

4. Easier Debugging:
   - Isolate exact test that fails
   - No cascade failures
   - Clear cause and effect

5. Simplified Test Writing:
   - No manual cleanup needed
   - Don't worry about previous test state
   - Focus on test logic

Manual Context Management:
--------------------------

When You Need Multiple Contexts in One Test:

Example: Testing Multi-User Scenarios

```
test('chat between two users', async ({ browser }) => {
  // Create two contexts
  const context1 = await browser.newContext();
  const context2 = await browser.newContext();
  
  // Create pages in each context
  const user1Page = await context1.newPage();
  const user2Page = await context2.newPage();
  
  // User 1 logs in
  await user1Page.goto('/login');
  await user1Page.fill('#username', 'user1');
  await user1Page.fill('#password', 'pass1');
  await user1Page.click('#login');
  
  // User 2 logs in (separate session)
  await user2Page.goto('/login');
  await user2Page.fill('#username', 'user2');
  await user2Page.fill('#password', 'pass2');
  await user2Page.click('#login');
  
  // User 1 sends message
  await user1Page.goto('/chat');
  await user1Page.fill('#message', 'Hello!');
  await user1Page.click('#send');
  
  // User 2 receives message
  await user2Page.goto('/chat');
  await expect(user2Page.locator('.message')).toHaveText('Hello!');
  
  // Cleanup
  await context1.close();
  await context2.close();
});
```

Context Options:
----------------

Creating Custom Context:

```
const context = await browser.newContext({
  viewport: { width: 1920, height: 1080 },
  userAgent: 'My Custom User Agent',
  locale: 'en-US',
  timezoneId: 'America/New_York',
  permissions: ['geolocation'],
  geolocation: { latitude: 40.7128, longitude: -74.0060 },
  colorScheme: 'dark',
  deviceScaleFactor: 2,
  hasTouch: true,
  isMobile: true,
});
```

Saving and Reusing Context State:
----------------------------------

Save State (e.g., after login):

```
test.beforeAll(async ({ browser }) => {
  const context = await browser.newContext();
  const page = await context.newPage();
  
  // Login
  await page.goto('/login');
  await page.fill('#username', 'testuser');
  await page.fill('#password', 'password');
  await page.click('#login');
  
  // Save state
  await context.storageState({ path: 'auth.json' });
  await context.close();
});
```

Reuse State:

```
test.use({ storageState: 'auth.json' });

test('already logged in', async ({ page }) => {
  // Already authenticated!
  await page.goto('/dashboard');
  await expect(page.locator('.welcome')).toBeVisible();
});
```

Context vs Page vs Browser:
----------------------------

Browser:
- The actual browser instance (Chromium/Firefox/WebKit)
- Can have multiple contexts
- Expensive to create

Context:
- Isolated browsing session
- Can have multiple pages (tabs)
- Cheap to create
- Playwright creates automatically per test

Page:
- Single tab/page within a context
- Where you interact with content
- Playwright provides via fixture

Relationship:
```
Browser (one per worker)
├── Context (one per test, auto-created)
│   └── Page (one per test, auto-provided)
├── Context (next test)
│   └── Page
└── Context (next test)
    └── Page
```

Advanced: When NOT to Use Isolation:
-------------------------------------

Sometimes you want shared state across tests:

Use Case: Expensive Setup

```
test.describe('Dashboard tests', () => {
  let sharedContext;
  let sharedPage;
  
  test.beforeAll(async ({ browser }) => {
    // One-time expensive setup
    sharedContext = await browser.newContext();
    sharedPage = await sharedContext.newPage();
    
    // Login once
    await sharedPage.goto('/login');
    await sharedPage.fill('#username', 'user');
    await sharedPage.fill('#password', 'pass');
    await sharedPage.click('#login');
  });
  
  test('view dashboard', async () => {
    await sharedPage.goto('/dashboard');
    await expect(sharedPage.locator('h1')).toHaveText('Dashboard');
  });
  
  test('view profile', async () => {
    await sharedPage.goto('/profile');
    await expect(sharedPage.locator('h1')).toHaveText('Profile');
  });
  
  test.afterAll(async () => {
    await sharedContext.close();
  });
});
```

⚠️ Trade-off: Speed vs. Isolation
Use only when setup is genuinely expensive and tests are read-only!

================================================================================
PART II: CORE FEATURES AND PRACTICES
================================================================================

9. FIXTURES AND DEPENDENCY INJECTION
=====================================

What Are Fixtures?
------------------

Definition:
Fixtures are pre-configured test dependencies that Playwright automatically
provides to your tests. Think of them as "ingredients" that are prepared and
handed to each test, then cleaned up automatically.

The Magic of { page }:
----------------------

When you write:
```
test('example', async ({ page }) => {
  // page is ready to use!
});
```

Behind the scenes, Playwright:
1. Creates a new browser context
2. Creates a new page within that context
3. Injects the page into your test function
4. Waits for your test to complete
5. Closes the page
6. Closes the context
7. Cleans up all resources

This is Dependency Injection in action!

Built-in Fixtures:
------------------

1. page: Page
   - Most commonly used fixture
   - Fresh page for each test
   - Auto-cleanup after test
   
   Usage:
   ```
   test('use page', async ({ page }) => {
     await page.goto('https://example.com');
     await page.click('#button');
   });
   ```

2. context: BrowserContext
   - When you need multiple pages
   - Share state across pages
   - Custom context settings
   
   Usage:
   ```
   test('multiple pages', async ({ context }) => {
     const page1 = await context.newPage();
     const page2 = await context.newPage();
     
     await page1.goto('https://example.com');
     await page2.goto('https://example.com/other');
     
     // Both pages share same context (cookies, storage)
   });
   ```

3. browser: Browser
   - Low-level browser access
   - Create custom contexts
   - Advanced scenarios
   
   Usage:
   ```
   test('custom context', async ({ browser }) => {
     const context = await browser.newContext({
       viewport: { width: 1920, height: 1080 },
       locale: 'fr-FR',
     });
     
     const page = await context.newPage();
     await page.goto('https://example.com');
   });
   ```

4. request: APIRequestContext
   - API testing
   - HTTP requests without browser
   - Faster than browser for API calls
   
   Usage:
   ```
   test('API test', async ({ request }) => {
     const response = await request.get('https://api.example.com/users');
     expect(response.status()).toBe(200);
     const data = await response.json();
     expect(data.users).toHaveLength(10);
   });
   ```

5. browserName: string
   - Current browser name
   - Conditional test logic
   - 'chromium', 'firefox', or 'webkit'
   
   Usage:
   ```
   test('conditional test', async ({ page, browserName }) => {
     if (browserName === 'webkit') {
       test.skip('Safari not supported');
     }
     await page.goto('https://example.com');
   });
   ```

Requesting Multiple Fixtures:
------------------------------

```
test('multiple fixtures', async ({ page, context, request, browserName }) => {
  console.log(`Testing on ${browserName}`);
  
  // Use API to setup data
  await request.post('https://api.example.com/setup', {
    data: { user: 'testuser' }
  });
  
  // Use page for UI
  await page.goto('https://example.com');
  
  // Create additional page in same context
  const page2 = await context.newPage();
  await page2.goto('https://example.com/other');
});
```

Custom Fixtures:
----------------

Creating Your Own Fixtures:

Basic Example:
```
import { test as base, expect } from '@playwright/test';

type MyFixtures = {
  todoPage: Page;
};

export const test = base.extend<MyFixtures>({
  todoPage: async ({ page }, use) => {
    // Setup: Navigate to todo page
    await page.goto('https://demo.playwright.dev/todomvc');
    
    // Provide to test
    await use(page);
    
    // Teardown: Optional cleanup
    // (page is auto-closed, but you could clear todos, etc.)
  },
});

export { expect };
```

Use in tests:
```
import { test, expect } from './my-fixtures';

test('add todo', async ({ todoPage }) => {
  // todoPage is already on the todo page!
  await todoPage.fill('.new-todo', 'Buy milk');
  await todoPage.press('.new-todo', 'Enter');
  
  await expect(todoPage.locator('.todo-list li')).toHaveText('Buy milk');
});
```

Advanced Custom Fixture - Authenticated User:
```
import { test as base, Page } from '@playwright/test';

type AuthFixtures = {
  authenticatedPage: Page;
};

export const test = base.extend<AuthFixtures>({
  authenticatedPage: async ({ page }, use) => {
    // Login before each test
    await page.goto('https://example.com/login');
    await page.fill('#username', process.env.TEST_USER || 'testuser');
    await page.fill('#password', process.env.TEST_PASSWORD || 'password');
    await page.click('#login');
    
    // Wait for login to complete
    await page.waitForURL('**/dashboard');
    
    // Provide authenticated page to test
    await use(page);
    
    // Logout after test (optional)
    await page.click('#logout');
  },
});

export { expect };
```

Usage:
```
test('view dashboard', async ({ authenticatedPage }) => {
  // Already logged in!
  await expect(authenticatedPage.locator('h1')).toHaveText('Dashboard');
});

test('edit profile', async ({ authenticatedPage }) => {
  // Already logged in!
  await authenticatedPage.click('a[href="/profile"]');
  await authenticatedPage.fill('#name', 'New Name');
  await authenticatedPage.click('#save');
});
```

Fixture Scope:
--------------

test-scoped (default):
- New instance for each test
- Most common
- Complete isolation

```
export const test = base.extend<MyFixtures>({
  myFixture: async ({}, use) => {
    // Runs before EACH test
    const resource = await setupResource();
    await use(resource);
    // Runs after EACH test
    await cleanupResource();
  },
});
```

worker-scoped:
- Shared across tests in same worker
- Setup once per worker
- Faster but less isolated

```
export const test = base.extend<MyFixtures>({
  myFixture: [async ({}, use) => {
    // Runs ONCE per worker
    const resource = await setupExpensiveResource();
    await use(resource);
    // Runs ONCE after all tests in worker
    await cleanupResource();
  }, { scope: 'worker' }],
});
```

Real-World Example - Database Fixture:
```
type DbFixtures = {
  db: Database;
};

export const test = base.extend<DbFixtures>({
  db: [async ({}, use) => {
    // Connect once per worker
    const db = await connectToDatabase();
    await use(db);
    // Disconnect after all tests
    await db.close();
  }, { scope: 'worker' }],
});

// In tests:
test('create user', async ({ db, page }) => {
  const userId = await db.insert('users', { name: 'Test User' });
  
  await page.goto(`/users/${userId}`);
  await expect(page.locator('.username')).toHaveText('Test User');
  
  await db.delete('users', userId);
});
```

Fixture Dependencies:
---------------------

Fixtures can depend on other fixtures:

```
type MyFixtures = {
  adminPage: Page;
  regularPage: Page;
};

export const test = base.extend<MyFixtures>({
  adminPage: async ({ page }, use) => {
    await page.goto('/login');
    await page.fill('#username', 'admin');
    await page.fill('#password', 'admin123');
    await page.click('#login');
    await use(page);
  },
  
  regularPage: async ({ page }, use) => {
    await page.goto('/login');
    await page.fill('#username', 'user');
    await page.fill('#password', 'user123');
    await page.click('#login');
    await use(page);
  },
});
```

Complex Fixture - Page Object:
```
import { LoginPage } from './pages/LoginPage';
import { DashboardPage } from './pages/DashboardPage';

type PageFixtures = {
  loginPage: LoginPage;
  dashboardPage: DashboardPage;
};

export const test = base.extend<PageFixtures>({
  loginPage: async ({ page }, use) => {
    const loginPage = new LoginPage(page);
    await use(loginPage);
  },
  
  dashboardPage: async ({ page }, use) => {
    const dashboardPage = new DashboardPage(page);
    await use(dashboardPage);
  },
});

// Usage:
test('login and navigate', async ({ loginPage, dashboardPage }) => {
  await loginPage.login('user', 'pass');
  await dashboardPage.expectWelcomeMessage('Welcome, user!');
});
```

Automatic Fixtures (Auto):
---------------------------

Fixtures that run automatically without being requested:

```
export const test = base.extend<{}, { autoLogin: void }>({
  autoLogin: [async ({ page }, use) => {
    // Runs before EVERY test automatically
    await page.goto('/login');
    await page.fill('#username', 'auto-user');
    await page.fill('#password', 'auto-pass');
    await page.click('#login');
    
    await use();
    
    // Cleanup automatically
  }, { auto: true }],
});

// No need to request autoLogin - it runs automatically!
test('test with auto login', async ({ page }) => {
  // Already logged in!
  await page.goto('/dashboard');
});
```

Best Practices:
---------------

1. Use Built-in Fixtures When Possible:
   ✓ { page } for most tests
   ✓ { request } for API tests
   ✓ { context } when you need multiple pages

2. Custom Fixtures for Repeated Setup:
   ✓ Authentication
   ✓ Data setup
   ✓ Page objects
   ✓ Complex configurations

3. Proper Scoping:
   ✓ test-scoped for isolation (default)
   ✓ worker-scoped for expensive setup (database connections, etc.)
   ✗ Don't overuse worker-scope - can cause flaky tests

4. Clear Naming:
   ✓ authenticatedPage
   ✓ adminUser
   ✓ testDatabase
   ✗ myFixture
   ✗ data
   ✗ stuff

5. Cleanup:
   ✓ Use try/finally if needed
   ✓ Most resources auto-cleanup (page, context)
   ✓ Explicitly close database connections, file handles

Common Patterns:
----------------

1. Test Data Fixture:
```
export const test = base.extend<{ testUser: User }>({
  testUser: async ({ request }, use) => {
    // Create test user via API
    const response = await request.post('/api/users', {
      data: { username: 'testuser', email: 'test@example.com' }
    });
    const user = await response.json();
    
    await use(user);
    
    // Cleanup: Delete test user
    await request.delete(`/api/users/${user.id}`);
  },
});
```

2. Storage State Fixture:
```
export const test = base.extend<{ authenticatedContext: BrowserContext }>({
  authenticatedContext: async ({ browser }, use) => {
    const context = await browser.newContext({
      storageState: 'auth.json'  // Pre-saved auth state
    });
    
    await use(context);
    await context.close();
  },
});
```

3. Mock Data Fixture:
```
export const test = base.extend<{ mockApi: void }>({
  mockApi: async ({ page }, use) => {
    await page.route('**/api/**', route => {
      route.fulfill({
        status: 200,
        body: JSON.stringify({ users: [] })
      });
    });
    
    await use();
  },
});
```

================================================================================

10. LOCATORS AND SELECTORS
===========================

What Are Locators?
------------------

Locators are Playwright's way of finding elements on a page. Unlike traditional
selectors, locators are:

1. Lazy: Don't search until action is performed
2. Strict: Fail if multiple elements match
3. Auto-retrying: Wait for element to be actionable
4. Resilient: Handle dynamic content automatically

Traditional vs Playwright Approach:
------------------------------------

Selenium/WebDriver:
```
WebElement element = driver.findElement(By.id("button"));
element.click();
```
Problem: Element found once, might be stale later

Playwright:
```
const button = page.locator('#button');
await button.click();
```
Benefit: Searches fresh on each action, handles staleness

Creating Locators:
------------------

1. CSS Selectors:
```
page.locator('#id')                  // By ID
page.locator('.class')               // By class
page.locator('button')               // By tag
page.locator('[data-testid="btn"]') // By attribute
page.locator('button.primary')       // Combination
page.locator('div > button')         // Child combinator
page.locator('div button')           // Descendant
```

2. Text Content:
```
page.locator('text=Click me')              // Exact text
page.locator('text=Click')                 // Substring
page.getByText('Click me')                 // Recommended
page.getByText('Click me', { exact: true }) // Exact match
page.getByText(/click me/i)                // Regex (case-insensitive)
```

3. Role-based (Accessible):
```
page.getByRole('button', { name: 'Submit' })
page.getByRole('heading', { level: 1 })
page.getByRole('textbox', { name: 'Email' })
page.getByRole('link', { name: 'Learn more' })
page.getByRole('checkbox', { checked: true })
```

Available roles:
- button, link, textbox, checkbox, radio
- heading, listitem, option, tab, menuitem
- alert, dialog, navigation, main, etc.

4. Label-based:
```
page.getByLabel('Email address')
page.getByLabel('Remember me')
```

5. Placeholder:
```
page.getByPlaceholder('Enter your email')
```

6. Alt text:
```
page.getByAltText('User avatar')
```

7. Title:
```
page.getByTitle('Close')
```

8. Test ID (Recommended for Testing):
```
page.getByTestId('submit-button')
```
Requires: data-testid attribute in HTML
```html
<button data-testid="submit-button">Submit</button>
```

Locator Methods:
----------------

Filtering Locators:
```
// Filter by text
page.locator('button').filter({ hasText: 'Submit' })

// Filter by another locator
page.locator('article').filter({ has: page.locator('.featured') })

// Exclude
page.locator('button').filter({ hasNotText: 'Cancel' })
```

Chaining Locators:
```
page.locator('.container')
    .locator('.list')
    .locator('li')
    .filter({ hasText: 'Item 3' })
```

Getting Specific Element:
```
page.locator('li').first()           // First match
page.locator('li').last()            // Last match
page.locator('li').nth(2)            // Zero-indexed (3rd element)
```

Counting Elements:
```
const count = await page.locator('li').count();
console.log(`Found ${count} items`);
```

Getting All Elements:
```
const items = await page.locator('li').all();
for (const item of items) {
  console.log(await item.textContent());
}
```

Locator Actions:
----------------

Common Actions (auto-wait for element to be actionable):

```
await locator.click()                     // Click
await locator.dblclick()                  // Double-click
await locator.fill('text')                // Fill input
await locator.type('text', { delay: 100 }) // Type with delay
await locator.press('Enter')              // Press key
await locator.check()                     // Check checkbox
await locator.uncheck()                   // Uncheck checkbox
await locator.selectOption('value')       // Select dropdown
await locator.hover()                     // Hover
await locator.focus()                     // Focus element
await locator.clear()                     // Clear input
await locator.setInputFiles('file.pdf')   // Upload file
await locator.dragTo(targetLocator)       // Drag and drop
```

Click Options:
```
await locator.click({ button: 'right' })         // Right-click
await locator.click({ modifiers: ['Control'] })  // Ctrl+Click
await locator.click({ position: { x: 10, y: 10 } }) // Click at position
await locator.click({ force: true })             // Force click (bypass checks)
await locator.click({ clickCount: 2 })           // Double-click
```

Extracting Information:
------------------------

```
await locator.textContent()              // Text content
await locator.innerText()                // Visible text
await locator.innerHTML()                // HTML content
await locator.getAttribute('href')       // Attribute value
await locator.inputValue()               // Input field value
await locator.isVisible()                // Visibility check
await locator.isEnabled()                // Enabled check
await locator.isChecked()                // Checkbox state
await locator.isEditable()               // Editable check
await locator.isDisabled()               // Disabled check
await locator.isHidden()                 // Hidden check
await locator.boundingBox()              // Element dimensions
await locator.screenshot()               // Screenshot of element
```

Assertions with Locators:
--------------------------

Playwright's expect works seamlessly with locators:

```
await expect(locator).toBeVisible()
await expect(locator).toBeHidden()
await expect(locator).toBeEnabled()
await expect(locator).toBeDisabled()
await expect(locator).toBeChecked()
await expect(locator).toBeEditable()
await expect(locator).toBeFocused()
await expect(locator).toHaveText('expected text')
await expect(locator).toContainText('partial')
await expect(locator).toHaveValue('input value')
await expect(locator).toHaveAttribute('href', '/page')
await expect(locator).toHaveClass('active')
await expect(locator).toHaveClass(/active/)
await expect(locator).toHaveCount(5)
await expect(locator).toHaveCSS('color', 'rgb(255, 0, 0)')
await expect(locator).toHaveId('submit')
```

Best Practices for Selectors:
------------------------------

Priority Order (Most to Least Recommended):

1. ✅ Role-based selectors (Most resilient):
   ```
   page.getByRole('button', { name: 'Submit' })
   ```
   - Accessible by default
   - Works with screen readers
   - Resistant to style changes

2. ✅ Test ID (Best for testing):
   ```
   page.getByTestId('submit-btn')
   ```
   - Never changes
   - Clear intent
   - No coupling to implementation

3. ✅ Label/Placeholder (User-facing):
   ```
   page.getByLabel('Email')
   page.getByPlaceholder('Enter email')
   ```
   - User perspective
   - Meaningful
   - Breaks if UX changes (good thing!)

4. ⚠️ Text content (Use carefully):
   ```
   page.getByText('Submit')
   ```
   - Breaks with text changes
   - Localization issues
   - OK for stable text

5. ⚠️ CSS selectors (Brittle):
   ```
   page.locator('.submit-button')
   ```
   - Breaks with style changes
   - Not semantic
   - Use only when necessary

6. ❌ XPath (Avoid):
   ```
   page.locator('xpath=//button[@class="submit"]')
   ```
   - Hard to read
   - Brittle
   - Slow
   - Use only as last resort

Real-World Examples:
--------------------

Login Form:
```
test('login', async ({ page }) => {
  await page.goto('/login');
  
  // Use labels (best for forms)
  await page.getByLabel('Email').fill('user@example.com');
  await page.getByLabel('Password').fill('password123');
  
  // Use role for button
  await page.getByRole('button', { name: 'Sign in' }).click();
  
  // Assert URL changed
  await expect(page).toHaveURL('/dashboard');
});
```

Navigation Menu:
```
test('navigate', async ({ page }) => {
  await page.goto('/');
  
  // Use role for navigation
  const nav = page.getByRole('navigation');
  await nav.getByRole('link', { name: 'Products' }).click();
  
  await expect(page).toHaveURL('/products');
  await expect(page.getByRole('heading', { level: 1 }))
    .toHaveText('Our Products');
});
```

Data Table:
```
test('table data', async ({ page }) => {
  await page.goto('/users');
  
  const table = page.getByRole('table');
  const rows = table.locator('tbody tr');
  
  // Check row count
  await expect(rows).toHaveCount(10);
  
  // Find specific row by text
  const userRow = rows.filter({ hasText: 'john@example.com' });
  
  // Click edit button in that row
  await userRow.getByRole('button', { name: 'Edit' }).click();
});
```

Dynamic Lists:
```
test('todo list', async ({ page }) => {
  await page.goto('/todos');
  
  // Add todos
  const input = page.getByPlaceholder('What needs to be done?');
  await input.fill('Buy milk');
  await input.press('Enter');
  await input.fill('Walk dog');
  await input.press('Enter');
  
  // Check count
  const todos = page.getByTestId('todo-item');
  await expect(todos).toHaveCount(2);
  
  // Complete first todo
  await todos.first().getByRole('checkbox').check();
  
  // Verify completed
  await expect(todos.first()).toHaveClass(/completed/);
});
```

Handling Multiple Elements:
----------------------------

Iterate Through Elements:
```
const products = page.locator('.product');
const count = await products.count();
```
